Fully smooth one shot multipartite soft covering of quantum states without pairwise independence
Pranab Sen

Abstract
We provide a powerful machinery to prove fully smooth one shot multipartite covering, aka convex split, type results for quantum states. In the important case of smooth multipartite convex split for classical quantum states, aka smooth multipartite soft covering, our machinery works even when certain marginals of these states do not satisfy pairwise independence. The paper [ Sen24 ] gave the first proof of fully smooth multipartite convex split by simplifying and extending a technique called telescoping, developed originally for convex split by [ CGB23 ] . However, that work as well as all earlier works on convex split assumed pairwise or even more independence amongst suitable marginals of the quantum states. We develop our machinery by leveraging known results from [ Sen21b ] involving tilting and augmentation smoothing of quantum states, combined with a novel observation that a natural quantum operation ‘flattening’ quantum states actually preserves the fidelity. This machinery is powerful enough to lead to non pairwise independent results as mentioned above. As an application of our soft covering lemma without pairwise independence, we prove the ‘natural’ one shot inner bounds for sending private classical information over a quantum wiretap interference channel, even when the classical encoders at the input lose pairwise independence in their encoding strategies to a certain extent. This result was unknown earlier even in the classical setting.



1
Introduction

The simultaneous smoothing bottleneck is a famous open problem in
network quantum
information theory
[
DF13
]
. A positive result for
this problem would imply
that many union and intersection type arguments carried out, sometimes
implicitly, in network classical information theory extend similarly
to the quantum setting. Most tasks in information theory can be decomposed
into simpler tasks of one of two types:
packing
and

covering
. The earlier work of
[
Sen21b
]
gave a machinery
for implementing union and intersection for packing tasks in one shot
network quantum information theory, bypassing simulaneous smoothing.
However it left open the question of implementing union and intersection
for covering tasks, a lacuna which was explicitly pointed out in

[
DGHW20
]
.

Recent exciting works of Cheng, Gao and
Berta
[
CGB23
]
, and Colomer and
Winter
[
CW23
]
, have introduced a telescoping cum
mean-zero decomposition technique that bypasses the simultaneous
smoothing bottleneck for intersection arguments for two fundamental
problems in quantum information theory viz. multipartite covering aka
multipartite convex split, and multipartite decoupling respectively.
However Cheng et al. did not state their
multipartite convex split
results in terms of smooth one shot quantities, leaving several
basic one shot and finite blocklength achievability questions
in network quantum information theory, e.g. inner bounds for the
generalised one shot quantum Slepian Wolf
[
AJW18
]
problem,
unanswered. Very recently,
[
Sen24
]
showed that
the telescoping technique can in fact be simplified and further extended
in order to prove fully smooth multipartite convex split and decoupling
results for the first time. That work also
provided several applications of the fully smooth convex
split and decoupling theorems to important problems in network
quantum information theory.

All the works referenced above as well as all earlier
works on convex split, whether smooth or non
smooth, assumed at least pairwise, often full independence
or tensor product structure of certain marginals of quantum states.
However, all the earlier proof techniques including telescoping
fail when the pairwise independence
requirement is dropped. Dropping pairwise independence becomes important
for classical quantum soft covering lemmas.
In multipartite soft covering, there are
k

classical parties,
k\geq 1
. Each party independently samples a set
of classical
symbols from a probability distribution. A
k
-tuple of
symbols from the samples of the
k
parties is fed to a box whose
output is a quantum state
depending on the input
k
-tuple. The uniform average of the
resulting input dependent quantum state is taken
over all
k
tuples that can be constructed from the
k
sets sampled
by the
k
parties. The aim is to ensure that the resulting
sample averaged input dependent quantum state is close in
trace distance to an ‘ideal’
fixed quantum state, in expectation over the random choices of the

k
parties. Dropping pairwise independence for soft covering means
that a classical party chooses its set of symbols in a pairwise
dependent fashion.

Soft covering lemmas
are used e.g. to prove the privacy requirement for classical messages
for various types of wiretap channels

[
RSW17
,
Wil17
]
.
Relaxing pairwise independence
in such settings can make computational sense as it will require
senders to use less true random bits, an expensive resource,
in their codebook construction step.

In this paper, we provide a new proof for fully smooth multipartite
convex split of quantum states. Our proof does not use telescoping;
instead it uses a completely different machinery. The advantage
of using this more sophisticated machinery is that in the
soft covering setting, we can show
for the first time that our fully
smooth inner bounds continue to hold
even when the classical covering parties lose pairwise independence
in a restricted way while choosing their set of samples. We believe
this is a major
conceptual contribution of our work.

In order to prove such powerful
and general multipartite fully quantum convex split and soft classical
quantum covering cresults, we develop new
machinery by leveraging known results from

[
Sen21b
]
involving tilting and augmentation smoothing
of quantum states, combined with a novel observation that a natural
quantum operation ‘flattening’ quantum states actually preserves the
fidelity.
As an application of the power of this machinery, we prove the
achievability
of a natural rate region for sending private classical information
over a wiretap quantum interference channel extending Chong, Motani,
Garg and ElGamal inner bound
[
CMGE08
]
for the non-wiretap
classical interference
channel. Our scheme continues to work even when the classical encodings
at the inputs to the channel lose pairwise independence to a certain
extent. This was not known before, not even in the classical setting.

1.1
Organisation of the paper

Section
2
serves as a warmup, where we first
recall the CMG covering problem introduced in
[
Sen24
]
.
That work provided a smooth inner bound for CMG covering using
telescoping. In Section
2
,
we will see how the telescoping proof of smooth CMG covering fails
if the pairwise independence
assumption between certain marginals of classical quantum states involved
in the telescoping proof is removed.
Some novel results about flattening quantum states,
Schatten-
\ell_{2}
norm and inner product of matrices, and tilting and
augmentation smoothing of quantum states
are stated and proved in Section
3
.
They will be required to prove fully
smooth multipartite convex split in Section
4
.
The style of proof of convex split in Section
4

serves as a warmup to the proof of the smooth multipartite soft covering
lemma without pairwise independence in
Section
5
.
Section
5
also proves a smooth
inner bound for the CMG covering problem that works even when
certain pairwise independence assumptions are relaxed, addressing
the drawback of telescoping pointed out earlier in
Section
2
.
We use this inner bound for CMG covering in
Section
6
, where
we finally
obtain smooth inner bounds for sending private classical information
over a quantum wiretap interference channel, even when certain
pairwise independence requirements at the classical encoders are relaxed.
Section
7
contains an alternate simpler
proof of fully smooth multipartite convex split using our flattening
technique.
It serves as a warmup to Section
8
, where we
prove a fully smooth multipartite decoupling theorem using similar
methods. The fully smooth multipartite decoupling theorem of
Section
8
uses
one extra ancilla qubit per sender unlike the fully smooth multipartite
decoupling theorem in
[
Sen24
]
; however this extra
qubit per sender does not affect any application of decoupling to the
best of our knowledge.
We finally make some concluding remarks and list directions for
further research
in Section
9
.

2
Warmup: Telescoping fails without pairwise
independence

The CMG covering problem defined in
[
Sen24
]
lies
at the heart of obtaining
inner bounds for private classical communication over a quantum
wiretap interference channel. The name CMG is an
acronym for Chong, Motani and Garg, the discoverers of one of the most
well known inner bounds for the classical non-wiretap interference channel
in the asympotic independent and identically distributed (asymptotic iid)
setting
[
CMGE08
]
. The paper
[
Sen12
]
first
showed the achievability of
the same inner bound for sending classical information over a quantum
non-wiretap interference channel in the asymptotic iid setting.
Subsequently, the achievability in the non-wiretap quantum case in
the general one shot setting was proved in
[
Sen21a
]
.
That paper used the machinery of
[
Sen21b
]
to bypass
simultaneous smoothing for intersection problems arising in packing
tasks. Later on in this paper, we will be able to extend the result of

[
Sen21a
]
to the one shot wiretap quantum case by
combining the packing arguments with our fully smooth CMG covering
lemma without pairwise independence
(Lemma
19
below.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a quantum channel with two inputs called Alice and Bob,
and one output called Eve. The intuition here is that Eve is an
eavesdropper trying to get
information on the classical messages that Alice and Bob are sending
to some other outputs of

\mathcal{N}
, not described here explicitly as they are not
relevant for CMG covering. Though
\mathcal{N}
has quantum inputs and quantum
outputs, we will assume without loss of generality that the inputs are
classical, because in the inner bound below, there is a standard
optimisation
step over a choice of all ensembles of input states to Alice and Bob.
Thus, we will henceforth think of
\mathcal{N}
as a classical-quantum (cq)
channel with its two classical input alphabets being denoted by

\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
, and the quantum output alphabet by
\mathcal{E}
.

Let
0\leq\epsilon\leq 1
.
On input
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, the channel outputs
a quantum state

\sigma^{E}_{xy}:=\mathcal{N}^{AB\rightarrow E}(\rho^{A}_{x}\otimes\rho^{B}_{y})

on
E
, where
\{\rho^{A}_{x}\}_{x}
,
\{\rho^{B}_{y}\}_{y}
are the ensembles of the
so-called encoding quantum states at the quantum inputs
A
,
B

of the channel which is
modelled by a completely positive trace non-increasing superoperator

\mathcal{N}:AB\rightarrow E
.
To define the CMG covering problem, we need to define new alphabets

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
, following the scheme of
the original paper
[
CMGE08
]
.
We then pick a ‘control’ probability distribution

p(x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y):=p(x^{\prime},x)p(y^{\prime},y)

on the classical alphabet

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{Y}
.
The ‘control’ cq state for the CMG covering problem is now defined as

\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}:=\sum_{x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y}p(x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y)|x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y\rangle\langle x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y|^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E}_{xy}.

Though the state
\sigma^{E}_{xy}
depends only on
x
and
y
, for
later convenience we will write it as

\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}:=\sigma^{E}_{xy}
. Though this notation seems
heavier now, it will be extremely useful when we take various marginals
of
\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}
in the entropic quantities and the proofs
later on. Thus,

\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}:=\sum_{x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y}p(x^{\prime},x)p(y^{\prime},y)|x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y\rangle\langle x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y|^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}.

Let us say Alice and Bob are trying to send a pair of classical messages

a
and
b
respectively to Charlie. To obfuscate them from Eve, Alice
and Bob independently do the following strategy.
Alice chooses iid samples
x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}}
from the
marginal distribution
p^{X^{\prime}}
.
Conditioned on each sample
x^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, Alice chooses iid
samples
x_{l^{\prime},1},\ldots,x_{l^{\prime},L}
from the conditioned
marginal distribution
p^{X}|(X^{\prime}=x^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}}
. Thus, a total of

L^{\prime}L
samples are chosen by Alice.
Similarly, Bob chooses iid samples
y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,y^{\prime}_{M^{\prime}}
from the
marginal distribution
p^{Y^{\prime}}
.
Conditioned on each sample
y^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
, Bob chooses iid
samples
y_{m^{\prime},1},\ldots,y_{m^{\prime},M}
from the conditioned
marginal distribution
p^{Y}|(Y^{\prime}=y^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}
. Thus, a total of

M^{\prime}M
samples are chosen by Bob.
Alice inputs a uniformly random sample from her chosen set into the
channel
\mathcal{N}
.
Similarly, Bob inputs a uniformly random sample from his chosen set
into the channel
\mathcal{N}
. The hope is that this strategy obfuscates
Eve’s received state so much that she is unable to figure out
the actual input message pair
(a,b)
.

Let
\sigma^{E}
denote the marginal on
E
of the control state

\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}
. Define

\sigma^{E}_{\vec{x^{\prime}},\vec{x},\vec{y^{\prime}},\vec{y}}:=(L^{\prime}(L+1)M^{\prime}(M+1))^{-1}\sum_{a^{\prime}=1}^{L^{\prime}}\sum_{a=1}^{L}\sum_{b^{\prime}=1}^{M^{\prime}}\sum_{b=1}^{M}\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}y_{b^{\prime}b}}.

(1)

We would like to show that the so-called CMG convex split quantity
defined below is small, when

L^{\prime}
,
L
,
M^{\prime}
,
M
are suitably large.

\mathrm{CMG}:=\E_{\vec{x^{\prime}},\vec{x},\vec{y^{\prime}},\vec{y}}[\|\sigma^{E}_{\vec{x^{\prime}},\vec{x},\vec{y^{\prime}},\vec{y}}-\sigma^{E}\|_{1}],

(2)

the expectation being over the choice of the samples

\vec{x^{\prime}}:=(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}})
,

\vec{x}:=(x_{1,1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{L^{\prime},L})
,

\vec{y^{\prime}}:=(y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,y^{\prime}_{M^{\prime}})
,

\vec{y}:=(y_{1,1},\ldots,y^{\prime}_{M^{\prime},M})
according to the procedure
described above. Recall that for
p>0
, the

Schatten-
\ell_{p}
norm
of a matrix
M
is defined as

\|M\|_{p}:=\Tr[(A^{\dagger}A)^{p/2}].

The
Schatten-
\ell_{\infty}
norm
, aka operator norm induced from the
Hilbert space norm, is defined by taking
p\rightarrow+\infty
in the
above expression, resulting in

\|M\|_{\infty}
being the largest singular value of
M
. For normal
matrices
M
, it equals the largest absolute value of an
eigenvalue of
M
.

The paper
[
Sen24
]
gave a fully smooth inner bound for
the CMG covering problem using telescoping. The telescoping based proof
in that paper uses expressions like

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,\hat{x},y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right.\right.$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\left.(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right]\right],\end{array}

(3)

in order to bound the CMG quantity in Equation
2
above.
Above, a matrix like
\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)
is a perturbed version
of the matrix

\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}:=(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}\circ\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}:=(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2},

where
\sigma^{-1}
is the so-called Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse of

\sigma
i.e. the inverse on the support of
\sigma
and the zero
operator orthogonal to the support.
Eight different perturbed versions of
\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}
called

\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(i)
,
i\in[8]
, are used
at different places of the telescoping proof

[
Sen24
]

in order to prove fully smooth inner bounds for
CMG covering.

The telescoping technique crucially requires that a telescoping term
satisfy
the so-called
mean zero property

[
Sen24
]
.
For a term like in Equation
3

above, various conditions need to be met in order for the mean
zero requirement to be satisfied. One of those conditions is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,\hat{x},y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right.\right.$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\left.(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right]\right]\\
&=&\E_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[\left(\E_{x|x^{\prime}}[\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)]\right)\right.\right.\\
&&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\left.\left(\E_{\hat{x}|x^{\prime}}[\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)]\right)\right]\right]\\
&=&\E_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right.\right.\\
&&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\left.(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right]\right]\\
&=&0.\end{array}

(4)

The equalities in the above equation only hold when in the obfuscating
strategy of Alice described earlier,
the distribution of
x_{a^{\prime}a}
conditioned
on
x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
is pairwise independent from the distribution of

x_{a^{\prime}\hat{a}}
conditioned on
x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
for any pair
a\neq\hat{a}
.

We can now appreciate the minimum requirement of pairwise independence
in the proofs of both non-smooth as well as smooth covering
lemmas.
Supppose the pairwise independence condition in Alice’s obfuscating
strategy is ‘slightly broken’. The mean zero
property in Equation
4
fails.
By
slightly broken
we mean that conditioned on a particular
choice of
x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
,
x_{a^{\prime}a}
,
y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}
,
y_{b^{\prime}b}
,

\E_{x_{a^{\prime}\hat{a}}|(x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}y_{b^{\prime}b})}[\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}\hat{a}}y^{\prime}_{b}y_{b^{\prime}b}}]=:\dot{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b}y_{b^{\prime}b}}\neq\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}_{b}y_{b^{\prime}b}},~~\dot{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b}y_{b^{\prime}b}}\leq\epsilon^{-1}\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}_{b}y_{b^{\prime}b}}.

We require that the expectation above depend only on the actual symbols

x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
,
x_{a^{\prime}a}
,
y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}
,
y_{b^{\prime}b}
and not on their locations

a^{\prime}
,
a
,
\hat{a}
,
b^{\prime}
or
b
.

This creates immense problems for telescoping. Suppose for simplicity
that only the
pairwise independence condition involving
X^{\prime}
and
X
is
slightly broken as described above i.e. conditioned on
x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
,
the distribution of
x_{a^{\prime}a}
is not independent from the distribution
of
x_{a^{\prime}\hat{a}}
for
a\neq\hat{a}
. Other pairs like

(x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}},x_{a^{\prime}a})
and
(x^{\prime}_{\hat{a^{\prime}}},x_{\hat{a^{\prime}}\hat{a}})

for
a^{\prime}\neq\hat{a^{\prime}}
, or
y_{b^{\prime}b}
and
y_{b^{\prime}\hat{b}}
conditioned
on
y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}
for
b\neq\hat{b}
etc. continue
to be independent.
Now writing a term like

(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}y_{b^{\prime}b}}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}y_{b^{\prime}b}}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}}(8))

in the telescoping inequalities, requires for example the following
condition (and several other conditions also) to be met in order
to satisfy the mean zero property. The following condition occurs
in the telescoping proof of
[
Sen24
]
when that proof
considers the mean zero requirement for the case

a^{\prime}=\hat{a^{\prime}}
,
a\neq\hat{a}
,

b^{\prime}=\hat{b^{\prime}}
,
b=\hat{b}
,

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,\hat{x},y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[\left(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\left.\left(\E_{\hat{x}|(x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y)}[\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)]\right)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))(\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right]\right]

\displaystyle\neq

\displaystyle 0,

which destroys the mean zero property.
On the other hand, writing a term like

(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}y_{b^{\prime}b}}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}y_{b^{\prime}b}}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}}(8)+\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}}(8))

in the telescoping inequalities gives an expression like
the following to meet the mean zero requirement in the proof of

[
Sen24
]
, when
a^{\prime}=\hat{a^{\prime}}
,
a\neq\hat{a}
,

b^{\prime}=\hat{b^{\prime}}
,
b=\hat{b}
,

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,\hat{x},y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}}(8)+\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[\left(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\left.\left(\E_{\hat{x}|(x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y)}[\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}}(8)+\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8)]\right)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y}\left[\Tr\left[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))(\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}(8)-\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}}(8)+\dot{\tilde{\sigma}}^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(8))\right]\right]

\displaystyle\neq

\displaystyle 0,

which again destroys the mean zero property. In fact, there is no
choice of term one could write down for a successful telescoping strategy
when pairwise
independence is slightly broken.

The proof of the non-smooth inner bound for CMG covering in

[
Sen24
]

handles the loss of pairwise independence
without much trouble giving rise to an inequality like

\log L^{\prime}+\log M^{\prime}+\log M>D_{\infty}(\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})+\log\epsilon^{-2},

(5)

in the inner bound.
Above, we have used the

non-smooth Rényi-
\infty
divergence
, aka non-smooth max
divergence. This and the
non-smooth Rényi-
2
divergence
are
defined below.

Definition 1

\begin{array}[]{c}I_{2}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}:=D_{2}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma^{E}),\\
I_{\infty}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}:=D_{\infty}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma^{E}),\\
D_{2}(\alpha\|\beta):=2\log\|\beta^{-1/4}\alpha\beta^{-1/4}\|_{2},~~\mbox{if $\mathrm{supp}(\alpha)\leq\mathrm{supp}(\beta)$, $+\infty$ otherwise},\\
D_{\infty}(\alpha\|\beta):=\log\|\beta^{-1/2}\alpha\beta^{-1/2}\|_{\infty},~~\mbox{if $\mathrm{supp}(\alpha)\leq\mathrm{supp}(\beta)$, $+\infty$ otherwise}.\end{array}

where
\alpha
is a
subnormalised density matrix
and

\beta
is a positive
semidefinite matrix acting on the same Hilbert space.
The quantity
I_{\infty}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}
is known as

non-smooth Rényi-
\infty
mutual information
, aka
non-smooth max mutual information under the joint state
\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}
.
The quantity
I_{2}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}
is known as the

non-smooth Rényi-2 mutual information

under the joint state
\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}
.
In this paper,
a subnormalised density matrix means a positive semidefinite matrix with
trace at most one; a
normalised density matrix
means the trace is
exactly one. Note that

D_{2}(\alpha\|\beta)\leq D_{\infty}(\alpha\|\beta)
, and that
for a cq state
\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}
,

I_{2}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}=\log\E_{x^{\prime}}\Tr[((\sigma^{E})^{-1/4}\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/4})^{2}]=\log\E_{x^{\prime}}\Tr[(\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}})^{2}],

the expecation over
x^{\prime}
being taken according to the classical probability
distribution
\sigma^{X^{\prime}}
.
For later use, we remark that for a cq state
\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}
,

I_{\infty}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}=\max_{x^{\prime}}D_{\infty}(\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}}\|\sigma^{E}).

The rate inequality in Equation
5
above
arises from a term like the one below, when the proof of the non-smooth
inner bound for CMG covering in
[
Sen24
]
considers a
situation where

a^{\prime}=\hat{a^{\prime}}
,
b^{\prime}=\hat{b^{\prime}}
,
b=\hat{b}
and

a\neq\hat{a}
:

\displaystyle\E_{\vec{x^{\prime}},\vec{x},\vec{y^{\prime}},\vec{y}}\left[\Tr[\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}x_{a^{\prime}a}y^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}y_{b^{\prime}b}}\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}_{\hat{a^{\prime}}}x_{\hat{a^{\prime}}\hat{a}}y^{\prime}_{\hat{b^{\prime}}}y_{\hat{b^{\prime}}\hat{b}}}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime}x\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}[\Tr[\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}\tilde{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}]]\;=\;\E_{x^{\prime}x\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}[\Tr[\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}\left[\Tr\left[\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}\E_{\hat{x}|(x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y)}[\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}\hat{x}y^{\prime}y}](\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}[\Tr[\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}\dot{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}]]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{D_{\infty}(\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})}\cdot\E_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}[\Tr[\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}\sigma^{E}(\sigma^{E})^{-1/2}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{D_{\infty}(\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})}\cdot\E_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}[\Tr[\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}]]\;=\;2^{D_{\infty}(\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})},

where the cq state
\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}
is defined in the
natural fashion as

\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}YE}:=\sum_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}p(x^{\prime},x)p(y^{\prime},y)|x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y\rangle\langle x^{\prime},x,y^{\prime},y|^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\dot{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}.

Observe that if

\dot{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}=\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y},

which is
what we have in the pairwise independent case,
then,

D_{\infty}(\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})=D_{\infty}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E}).

Our non-smooth rate region then becomes exactly like the non-smooth
rate region for CMG covering in
[
Sen24
]
in the
pairwise independent case,
except for
D_{2}(\cdot\|\cdot)
in
[
Sen24
]
’s rate region
being replaced by
D_{\infty}(\cdot\|\cdot)
.
Now suppose

\forall x:\dot{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}<\epsilon^{-1}\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y},

i.e. the ‘consequences’ of pairwise independence hold up to
a ‘scale factor’ of
\epsilon^{-1}
. Then,

D_{\infty}(\dot{\sigma}^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}XY^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})\leq D_{\infty}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})+\log\epsilon^{-1}.

Under an appropriate simultaneous smoothing conjecture, such a term
should give rise to an inequality like

\log L^{\prime}+\log M^{\prime}+\log M>D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})+\log\epsilon^{-1}+\mathrm{polylog}(\epsilon^{-1}),

where
0<\epsilon<1
is the so-called
smoothing parameter
.
Above we used the smooth Rényi-
\infty
divergence

D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(\cdot\|\cdot)
.
The
\epsilon
-smooth Rényi divergences are defined as follows:

D_{2}^{\epsilon}(\alpha\|\beta):=\min_{\alpha^{\prime}\approx_{\epsilon}\alpha}D_{2}(\alpha^{\prime}\|\beta),~~D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(\alpha\|\beta):=\min_{\alpha^{\prime}\approx_{\epsilon}\alpha}D_{\infty}(\alpha^{\prime}\|\beta),

where the minimisation is over all subnormalised density matrices

\alpha^{\prime}
satisfying
\|\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha\|_{1}\leq\epsilon(\Tr\alpha)
.
The smooth mutual informations are defined accordingly.

I_{2}^{\epsilon}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}:=D_{2}^{\epsilon}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma^{E}),\\
I_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(X^{\prime}:E)_{\sigma}:=D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}E}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma^{E}).

We remark
that several alternate definitions of non-smooth and smooth Rényi
divergences have been provided in the literature. However the work
of
[
ABJT19
]
shows that all the smooth Rényi-
p

divergences for
p>1
are essentially equivalent to

D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}
up to tweaks in the smoothing parameter
\epsilon

and dimension independent additive corrections that are polynomial in

\log\epsilon^{-1}
. So henceforth, our fully smooth
convex split and soft covering
results shall be stated in terms of
D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}
or
quantities derived from
D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}
like
I^{\epsilon}_{\infty}
.

Another important entropic quantity derived from Rényi divergence
is conditional entropy.
The smooth conditional Rényi entropy is defined by

H_{2}^{\epsilon}(A|R)_{\rho}:=-D_{2}^{\epsilon}(\rho^{AR}\|\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{A}\otimes\rho^{R}),~~H_{\mathrm{min}}^{\epsilon}(A|R)_{\rho}:=-D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(\rho^{AR}\|\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{A}\otimes\rho^{R}).

Various alternate definitions of the smooth conditional
Rényi-2 and Rényi min entropy
have been given in the literature, but there all equivalent up to
minor tweaks in the smoothing parameter
\epsilon
and small dimension
independent additive terms polynomial in
\log\epsilon^{-1}
.
Also,
H_{2}^{\epsilon}(A|R)_{\rho}\approx H_{\mathrm{min}}^{\epsilon}(A|R)_{\rho}

[
ABJT19
]
.

Now contrast the above expression with the following viz.

\displaystyle\log L^{\prime}+\log M^{\prime}+\log M

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle I_{2}^{\epsilon}(X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}Y:E)_{\sigma}+\log\epsilon^{-2}\;=\;D_{2}^{\epsilon}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})+\log\epsilon^{-2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}YE}\|\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}Y}\otimes\sigma^{E})+\Theta(\log\epsilon^{-2})

obtained by telescoping in the pairwise independent case

[
Sen24
]
.
Thus, simultaenous smoothing is still potentially much more powerful
than telescoping when pairwise independence is slightly broken in the
sense of
\dot{\sigma}^{E}_{x^{\prime}xy^{\prime}y}
being bounded by

\sigma^{E}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}y}
by at most a scale factor
of
\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon^{-1})
.

As we will see in Section
5
, our
machinery allows us to
handle the slight breaking of pairwise independence in the sense described
above, achieving the bound in terms of
D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}
that one would
expect from simultaneous smoothing. We remark that sacrificing pairwise
independence may be computationally beneficial for Alice’s obfuscation
strategy, as it will require her to use a lesser number of pure random
bits.

3
Preliminaries

We now state some basic definitions and facts for completeness. First
is the well known
gentle measurement
lemma.

Fact 1

Let
\rho
be a normalised density matrix on a Hilbert space. Let

\Pi
be a POVM element the same space (i.e.
\hbox{\small l\kern-3.5pt\normalsize 0}\leq\Pi\leq\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}
)
satisfying
\Tr[\Pi\rho]\geq 1-\epsilon
. Then,

\|\rho-\sqrt{\Pi}\,\rho\,\sqrt{\Pi}\|_{1}<2\sqrt{\epsilon}.

If
\rho
is not normalised, we can still obtain a good gentle measurement
upper bound by rescaling by
\Tr\rho
.

The
Löwner partial order
on Hermitian matrices
A
,
B
on the
same Hilbert space is defined as
A\leq B
iff
B-A
is positive
semidefinite. With this notation, we sometimes write
A\geq 0
to
indicate that
A
is positive semidefinite.

Every Hermitian positive semidefinite matrix
\rho^{A}
on a Hilbert
space
A
, with complex entries, has a
length preserving
purification

\rho^{AR}
on an extended Hilbert space
A\otimes R
,
where
R
is another Hilbert space, often called the
reference
,
of sufficiently large dimension. By a length preserving purification,
we mean that
\rho^{AR}
is a rank one orthogonal projection, i.e. a

pure state
, whose of same Schatten-
\ell_{1}
norm as that of

\rho^{A}
. Note that that the Schatten-
\ell_{1}
norm of a pure state

\rho^{AR}=|\rho\rangle\langle\rho|^{AR}
is the Euclidean
\ell_{2}
-length of
the vector
|\rho^{A}\rangle
.

Definition 2

The fidelity between two
positive semidefinite matrices
\rho^{A}
,
\sigma^{A}
on the same Hilbert
space
A
is defined as

F(\rho^{A},\sigma^{A}):=\|\sqrt{\rho}^{A}\sqrt{\sigma}^{A}\|_{1}=\max_{|\rho\rangle^{AR},|\sigma\rangle^{AR}}|\langle{\rho}|\sigma\rangle|,

where the maximisation is over all length preserving purifications

|\rho\rangle^{AR}
,
|\sigma\rangle^{AR}
of
\rho^{A}
,
\sigma^{A}
.

Given the above definition of fidelity, a straightforward analogue of
Uhlmann’s theorem holds for pairs of positive semidefinite matrices.
Also, we have the standard inequalities relating fidelity and trace
distance for normalised density matrices.

Fact 2

Let
\rho
,
\sigma
be normalised density matrices in the same Hilbert
space. Then,

1-F(\rho,\sigma)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\sigma\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{1-F(\rho,\sigma)^{2}}.

In this paper, all superoperators will be
Hermitian preserving
i.e.
they are linear operators on vector spaces of matrices that send
Hermitian matrices to Hermitian matrices. We will only apply superoperators
to Hermitian matrices inside our proofs.

We will need the notion of
‘intersection’ of two probability subdistributions
p^{X}
,
q^{X}
on the same
alphabet
X
from
[
Sen21b
]
.

(p\cap q)^{X}(x):=\min\{p(x),q(x)\}.

(6)

It is easy to see that

\|(p\cap q)^{X}-p^{X}\|_{1}\leq\|p^{X}-q^{X}\|_{1},~~\|(p\cap q)^{X}-q^{X}\|_{1}\leq\|p^{X}-q^{X}\|_{1}.

We state the following two folklore facts for completeness, which have
been commonly used in earlier literature on decoupling.

Fact 3

Let
\rho^{AB}
be a positive semidefinite matrix.
Then
\rho^{AB}\leq|A|(\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{A}\otimes\rho^{B})
.

Fact 4

Let
\rho^{AB}
be a subnormalised density matrix.
Then
H_{\mathrm{min}}^{\epsilon}(A|B)_{\rho}\leq\log|A|
.

We now recall the so-called

matrix weighted Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
. This inequality has
been extensively used in earlier literature on decoupling.

Fact 5

Let
M
be Hermitian matrix on a Hilbert space. Let
\sigma
be a
normalised density matrix on the same space such that

\mathrm{supp}(M)\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)
. Then,

\|M\|_{1}\leq\|\sigma^{-1/4}M\sigma^{-1/4}\|_{2},

where
\sigma^{-1}
is the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse of
\sigma
as
defined earlier. If
\Tr\sigma\neq 1
, still an appropriate inequality
can be easily obtained by rescaling by
\Tr\sigma
.

3.1
Shaved Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for matrix norms

Though the matrix weighted Cauchy Schwarz inequality is very useful,
it does have a notable drawback in certain applications. Suppose we want
to show an upper bound for
\|M_{1}-M_{2}\|_{1}
, where
M_{1}
,
M_{2}

are Hermitian matrices. In several applications, including during
the proof of the smooth multipartite
convex split lemma in the next section,
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
have incomparable
supports, and it is not clear what normalised density matrix
\sigma

should be used as the weighting matrix in
Fact
5
so as to get a good upper bound on

\|M_{1}-M_{2}\|_{1}
. For several of those applications, including for
our proof in the next section, it turns out that one can apply the
following
shaved Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
of
Proposition
1
in order to get
very good upper bounds on
\|M_{1}-M_{2}\|_{1}
. The term ‘shaved’ refers
to the use of the POVM element
\Pi
in
Proposition
1
which shaves off the ‘bad parts’
of
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
and applies Cauchy Schwarz only on the
good part
\sqrt{\Pi}(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})\sqrt{\Pi}
.

Proposition 1

Suppose
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
are two positive semidefinite matrices on the
same Hilbert space. Let
\Pi
be a POVM element on the same space.
Suppose
\Tr[\Pi\rho_{i}]\geq(1-\epsilon_{i})(\Tr\rho_{i})
. Then,

\|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\Tr\Pi}\cdot\|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\|_{2}+(2\Tr\rho_{1})\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}+(2\Tr\rho_{2})\sqrt{\epsilon_{2}}.

Proof:

We just apply Fact
1
and the standard Cauchy-Schwarz
inequality, together with the triangle inequality.

\displaystyle\|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\Pi}(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})\sqrt{\Pi}\|_{1}+\|\rho_{1}-\sqrt{\Pi}\,\rho_{1}\,\sqrt{\Pi}\|_{1}+\|\rho_{2}-\sqrt{\Pi}\,\rho_{2}\,\sqrt{\Pi}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Tr\Pi}\cdot\|\sqrt{\Pi}(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})\sqrt{\Pi}\|_{2}+(2\Tr\rho_{1})\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}+(2\Tr\rho_{2})\sqrt{\epsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Tr\Pi}\cdot\|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\|_{2}+(2\Tr\rho_{1})\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}+(2\Tr\rho_{2})\sqrt{\epsilon_{2}}.

\square

3.2
Projector smoothing of matrix
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm

As we will see below, our novel flattening lemmas reduce considerations
about
D_{\infty}(\alpha\|\beta)
to considerations about

\|\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
, where
\alpha^{\prime}
is a matrix related to
\alpha
,

\beta
. Accordingly, considerations about

D_{\infty}^{\epsilon}(\alpha\|\beta)
reduce to considerations about
a smoothed version of
\|\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
. Naively speaking, the smoothed
version of
\|\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
that arises from this reduction replaces
positive semidefinite matrix
\alpha^{\prime}
by a smoothed positive
semidefinite matrix
\hat{\alpha^{\prime}}
close to it leading to
the
state smoothed Schatten-
\ell_{\infty}
norm
.

\|\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{\epsilon}:=\min_{\hat{\alpha^{\prime}}}\|\hat{\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{\infty},

where the minimisation is over all positive semidefinite matrices

\hat{\alpha^{\prime}}
satisfying

\|\alpha^{\prime}-\hat{\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq\epsilon(\Tr\rho)
.
However for our purposes, as will become clear later, it is better to
smooth
\alpha^{\prime}
by applying a suitable orthogonal projector to it.
We call this approach
projector smoothing
as opposed to

state smoothing
. The following proposition relates the two
types of smoothing in the context of the Schatten-
\ell_{\infty}
norm.

Proposition 2

Let
0<\epsilon<1/4
.
Let
\rho
be a positive semidefinite matrix on a Hilbert space.
Let
\rho^{\prime}
be a positive
semidefinite matrix in the same space achieving the optimum in
the definition of
\|\rho\|_{\infty}^{\epsilon}
.
Then there exists an orthogonal projector in the same space such that

\Pi\rho=\rho\Pi,~~\Tr[\Pi\rho]\geq(1-\sqrt{\epsilon})(\Tr\rho),~~\|\Pi\rho\Pi\|_{\infty}\leq(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\|\rho^{\prime}\|_{\infty}.=(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\|\rho\|_{\infty}^{\epsilon}.

Proof:

Consider the matrices
\rho
,
\rho^{\prime}
expressed in the eigenbasis

\{|i\rangle\}_{i}
of
\rho
. Define the subset

\mbox{Bad}:=\{i:\rho(i,i)>(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\rho^{\prime}(i,i)\}.

Then,

\displaystyle\epsilon(\Tr\rho)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\rho-\rho^{\prime}\|_{1}\;\geq\;\sum_{i\in\mathrm{Bad}}(\rho(i,i)-\rho^{\prime}(i,i))\;\geq\;\sum_{i\in\mathrm{Bad}}\rho(i,i)(1-\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{\epsilon}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{1+2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{i\in\mathrm{Bad}},

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{i\in\mathrm{Bad}}\rho(i,i)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2}(\Tr\rho)\;<\;\sqrt{\epsilon}(\Tr\rho).

Let
\Pi
be the projector in the eigenbasis of
\rho
that exactly
skips
i\in\mbox{Bad}
. Then,

\Pi\rho=\rho\Pi,

\Tr[\Pi\rho]>(1-\sqrt{\epsilon})(\Tr\rho),

and

\|\Pi\rho\Pi\|_{\infty}=\max_{i\not\in\mathrm{Bad}}\rho(i,i)\leq\max_{i\not\in\mathrm{Bad}}(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\rho^{\prime}(i,i)\leq(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\|\rho^{\prime}\|_{\infty}.

This completes the proof of the proposition.

\square

3.3
From matrix
\|\cdot\|_{2}
norm to inner product

A crucial technique required in the proof of our
smooth multipartite convex split lemma is a technique to convert
the Schatten-
\ell_{2}
norm of a positive semidefinite matrix into
a Hilbert-Schmidt inner product of two related positive semidefinite
matrices.

Lemma 1

Let
0<\epsilon<1/4
.
Suppoose
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
are positive semidefinite operators on the
same Hilbert space. Let
\|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\|_{1}\leq\epsilon
.
Then there exists a positive semidefinite matrix
\rho^{\prime}_{1}
on the same
space such that

\rho^{\prime}_{1}\leq\rho_{1},~~\|\rho^{\prime}_{1}-\rho_{1}\|_{1}<\sqrt{\epsilon},~~\|\rho^{\prime}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\Tr[\rho_{1}\rho_{2}].

Proof:

We follow the lines of the proof of Proposition
2
,
and work in the eigenbasis
\{|i\rangle\}_{i}
of
\rho_{1}
.
Define the subset

\mbox{Bad}:=\{i:\rho_{1}(i,i)>(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\rho_{2}(i,i)\}\implies\sum_{i\in\mathrm{Bad}}\rho_{1}(i,i)<\sqrt{\epsilon}(\Tr\rho).

Define

\rho^{\prime}_{1}:=\sum_{i\not\in\mathrm{Bad}}\rho_{1}(i,i)|i\rangle\langle i|\implies\rho^{\prime}_{1}\leq\rho_{1},~~\|\rho_{1}-\rho^{\prime}_{1}\|_{1}<\sqrt{\epsilon}(\Tr\rho).

Now,

\displaystyle\|\rho^{\prime}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Tr[(\rho^{\prime}_{1})^{2}]\;=\;\sum_{i\not\in\mathrm{Bad}}\rho_{1}(i,i)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\sum_{i\not\in\mathrm{Bad}}\rho_{1}(i,i)\rho_{2}(i,i)\;\leq\;(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\sum_{i}\rho_{1}(i,i)\rho_{2}(i,i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})\Tr[\rho_{1}\rho_{2}].

This completes the proof of the lemma.

\square

3.4
Tilting and augmentation smoothing

In this subsection, we state some results about
tilting
and

augmentation smoothing
of quantum states from

[
Sen21b
]
, adapted to the specific requirements of this
paper.

Consider a normalised density matrix
\rho^{XYM}
. Introduce new
Hilbert spaces
L_{X}
,
L_{Y}
of sufficiently large (how large will
be clarified below) and equal dimension
L
. The
augmented state

is defined as

\rho^{(L_{X}X)(L_{Y}Y)M}:=\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{L_{X}}}{L}\otimes\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{L_{Y}}}{L}\otimes\rho^{XYM}.

As will become clear in Lemma
4
below, the
aim of augmentation is to reduce the
Schatten-
\ell_{\infty}
norm of

\rho^{L_{X}X}
,
\rho^{L_{Y}Y}
, which will play a crucial role in
a
smoothing property
to be stated below.

Introduce orthogonal copies of the Hilbert space
M
which we will
label as

\{M(l_{x})\}_{l_{x}\in[L]}
and

\{M(l_{y})\}_{l_{y}\in[L]}
. The orthogonal direct sum
of
M
together with all its copies is denoted by
\hat{M}
i.e.

\hat{M}:=M\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{l_{x}\in[L]}M(l_{x})\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{l_{y}\in[L]}M(l_{y})\right)\cong M\oplus(M\otimes L_{X})\oplus(M\otimes L_{Y}).

We interchangeably think of the augmented state as residing
in
L_{X}XL_{Y}YM
when we will write

\rho^{(L_{X}X)(L_{Y}Y)M}
, or as residing in
L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}
when
we will write
\rho^{(L_{X}X)(L_{Y}Y)\hat{M}}
.

Let
0<\epsilon<1
. Let
|m\rangle^{M}
denote a computational basis
state of
M
. For any fixed
l_{x}\in[L]
,
l_{y}\in[L]
,
the corresponding
tilting isometry
embedding
M
into

\hat{M}
is defined as

T^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}_{l_{x},l_{y},\epsilon}(|m\rangle^{M}):=\sqrt{1-2\epsilon}|m\rangle^{M}+\sqrt{\epsilon}|m(l_{x})\rangle^{M(l_{x})}+\sqrt{\epsilon}|m(l_{y})\rangle^{M(l_{y})},

with the extension to the entire vector space
M
by linearity.
Intuitively, tilting a vector means that we rotate it towards
an orthogonal direction by a small precise amount.

We next define some other tilting maps which are subnormally
scaled isometric embeddings of
M
into
\hat{M}
. For example,

T^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}_{l_{x},\epsilon}(|m\rangle^{M}):=\sqrt{1-2\epsilon}|m\rangle^{M}+\sqrt{\epsilon}|m(l_{x})\rangle^{M(l_{x})}.

Similarly, we define
T^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}_{l_{y},\epsilon}
. The
last two
of them are isometric embeddings of
M
into
\hat{M}
with scale factor

\sqrt{1-\epsilon}
.

Define another tilting map in the analogous fashion:

T^{L_{X}L_{Y}M\rightarrow L_{X}L_{Y}\hat{M}}_{\epsilon}(|l_{x}\rangle^{L_{X}}|l_{y}\rangle^{L_{X}}|m\rangle^{M}):=|l_{x}\rangle^{L_{X}}|l_{y}\rangle^{L_{Y}}\otimes T^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}_{l_{x},l_{y},\epsilon}(|m\rangle^{M}).

The tilting maps
T^{L_{X}M\rightarrow L_{X}\hat{M}}_{\epsilon}
,

T^{L_{Y}M\rightarrow L_{Y}\hat{M}}_{\epsilon}
are defined similarly.

Finally, we define the tilting map:

T^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YM\rightarrow L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}_{\epsilon}:=\mathbb{I}^{X}\otimes\mathbb{I}^{Y}\otimes T^{L_{X}L_{Y}M\rightarrow L_{X}L_{Y}\hat{M}}_{\epsilon}.

The tilting maps
T^{L_{X}XM\rightarrow L_{X}X\hat{M}}_{\epsilon}
,

T^{L_{Y}YM\rightarrow L_{Y}Y\hat{M}}_{\epsilon}
are then defined
similarly.
We remark that all the above tilting maps are isometric embeddings
with scale factors
\sqrt{1-\epsilon}
or
1
.
Also, all the tilting maps defined above
can be extended from spaces of
vectors to spaces of Hermitian matrices in the natural fashion, becoming
superoperators if need be.

We remark that the definition of tilting given above is slightly simpler
than the definition in
[
Sen21b
]
. This simplification
makes the proofs of subsequent statements much shorter; nevertheless
it is still strong enough for all known applications.

Let
\Pi^{XYM}(1)
,
\Pi^{XYM}(2)
,
\Pi^{XYM}(3)
be
certain orthogonal projectors in
XYM
satisfying

\Tr[\Pi^{XYM}(i)\rho^{XYM}]\geq 1-\sqrt{\epsilon},~~~\forall i\in[3].

(7)

In the context of smooth multipartite convex split, these projectors
will be related to optimising states
in the definitions of certain smooth entropic quantities.
We note that, in general, there is no relation amongst them.

Define the
\epsilon
-tilted approximate intersection
of the
projectors

\Pi^{XYM}(1)
,
\Pi^{XYM}(2)
,
\Pi^{XYM}(3)
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\hat{\Pi}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}&:=&\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}-\\
&&~~~\spanning\{(T^{L_{X}XM\rightarrow L_{X}X\hat{M}}_{\epsilon^{1/4}}\otimes\mathbb{I}^{L_{Y}Y})(\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{L_{X}L_{Y}}\otimes(\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{XYM}-\Pi^{XYM}(1))),\\
&&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(T^{L_{Y}YM\rightarrow L_{Y}Y\hat{M}}_{\epsilon^{1/4}}\otimes\mathbb{I}^{L_{X}X})(\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{L_{X}L_{Y}}\otimes(\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{XYM}-\Pi^{XYM}(2))),\\
&&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~T^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YM\rightarrow L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}_{\epsilon^{1/4}}(\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{L_{X}L_{Y}}\otimes(\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{XYM}-\Pi^{XYM}(3)))\},\end{array}

(8)

where the notation ‘span’ above means that we take the orthogonal
projection onto the vector space span of the supports of the
operators in the expression.
Intuitively, we are just defining intersection via
de Morgan’s rule i.e.
intersection is the complement of the union of complements.
Complement of a subspace, or rather of the orthogonal projection onto
that subspace, has a precise meaning in terms of matrices.
However, there is a problem defining the union of subspaces
as explained in more detail in
[
Sen21b
]
; naively
taking the span of subspaces often results in a catastrophe as it
can easily be the entire Hilbert space!
So, we need the
more sophisticated approach of
[
Sen21b
]
viz. tilt the
subspaces into orthogonal directions, then take their span, and then
take the complement.

Following
[
Sen21b
]
again, we now define a normalised
followed by a subnormalised density matrix. Both of them are
close to the augmented normalised density matrix
\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YM}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\hat{\rho}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}&:=&T^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YM\rightarrow L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}_{\epsilon^{1/4}}(\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YM}),\\
\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}&:=&\hat{\Pi}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}\circ\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}.\end{array}

(9)

These two states satisfy the following properties.

Lemma 2

\|\hat{\rho}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}-\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}\|_{1}=2\sqrt{2}\cdot\epsilon^{1/8}.

Proof:

Easily follows from the definition of the tilting isometry.

\square

Lemma 3

\Tr[\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}]\geq 1-121\epsilon^{1/4}.

Proof:(Sketch)

Easily follows from Equation
7
and
Proposition 3 of
[
Sen21b
]
regarding the
‘union’ properties of the so-called
matrix tilted span
.

\square

Corollary 1

\|\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}-\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}\|_{1}<22\cdot\epsilon^{1/8}.

Proof:

Follows by applying Fact
1
to
Lemma
3
.

\square

Corollary 2

\|\hat{\rho}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}-\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{L_{X}XL_{Y}Y\hat{M}}\|_{1}<25\cdot\epsilon^{1/8}.

Proof:

Triangle inequality and Lemma
2
and
Corollary
1
.

\square

We now state an important lemma that follows from the
smoothing properties of a tilted augmented state shown in

[
Sen21b
]
. It essentially states that tracing out

L_{X}
removes the tilt in
\hat{M}
along
l_{x}
in terms of the
Schatten
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm, and similarly for tracing out
L_{Y}
.

Lemma 4

For any
l_{x},l_{y}\in[L]
and positive semidefinite matrix
\sigma^{M}
,

\displaystyle\|L^{-1}\sum_{l^{\prime}_{y}\in[L]}T_{l_{x},l^{\prime}_{y},\epsilon^{1/4}}^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}(\sigma^{M})-T_{l_{x},\epsilon^{1/4}}^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}(\sigma^{M})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 4\epsilon^{1/8}L^{-1/2}(\Tr\sigma),

\displaystyle\|L^{-1}\sum_{l^{\prime}_{x}\in[L]}T_{l^{\prime}_{x},l_{y},\epsilon^{1/4}}^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}(\sigma^{M})-T_{l_{y},\epsilon^{1/4}}^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}(\sigma^{M})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 4\epsilon^{1/8}L^{-1/2}(\Tr\sigma),

\displaystyle\|L^{-2}\sum_{l^{\prime}_{x},l^{\prime}_{y}\in[L]}T_{l^{\prime}_{x},l^{\prime}_{y},\epsilon^{1/4}}^{M\rightarrow\hat{M}}(\sigma^{M})-\sigma^{M}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 8\epsilon^{1/8}L^{-1/2}(\Tr\sigma).

As a consequence, for any augmented normalised density matrix

\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YM}
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}^{L_{X}X\hat{M}}-T_{\epsilon^{1/4}}^{L_{X}XM\rightarrow L_{X}X\hat{M}}(\rho^{L_{X}XM})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 4\epsilon^{1/8}L^{-3/2},

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}^{L_{Y}Y\hat{M}}-T_{\epsilon^{1/4}}^{L_{Y}YM\rightarrow L_{Y}Y\hat{M}}(\rho^{L_{Y}YM})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 4\epsilon^{1/8}L^{-3/2},

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}^{\hat{M}}-\rho^{M}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 8\epsilon^{1/8}L^{-1/2}.

Proof:

Follows from Proposition 4 of
[
Sen21b
]
.

\square

3.5
Flattening quantum states

In this subsection, we shall show that a natural quantum operation
called
flattening
, aka the ‘brother construction’,
actually preserves the fidelity between quantum
states. The
brother construction
is a well known argument
in classical probability theory that is sometimes used to flatten a
specific probability distribution
by enlarging its support. It works as follows: Suppose
p^{X}
is a
probability distribution on the alphabet
X
. Assume for simplicity
that probability of each symbol in
X
is a rational number with
denominator
F_{p}
. We note that taking
F_{p}
to be large enough
approximates
p^{X}
quite well; this point will be made more precise
(and also quantum!) when we actually state our technical lemmas below.
So for now we assume that
p(x)=\frac{a_{x}}{F_{p}}
for all
x\in X
.
We take a new alphabet
L
large enough, and define a probability
distribution
p^{X}
on
XL
as follows:

p^{XL}(x,l):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\frac{1}{F_{p}}&l\leq a_{x},\\
&\\
0&l>a_{x}.\end{array}\right.

It is now easy to see that if
p^{XL}(x,l)\neq 0
for some
(x,l)
,
then
p^{XL}(x,l)=\frac{1}{F_{p}}
. In other words,
p^{XL}
is

flat on its support
. For a fixed
x
, the set of pairs

\{(x,l):l\leq a_{x}\}
are called the ‘brothers’ of
x
.

Note that the above brothers construction was tailored for a specific
probability distribution
p^{X}
. It will flatten only
p^{X}
. A lesser
known fact about the construction is that flattening according to

p^{X}
nevertheless preserves the
\ell_{1}
-distance between any
two probability distributions
q_{1}^{X}
,
q_{2}^{X}
. Flattening sometimes
leads to cleaner proofs in classical probability theory. As we will
see later in our proof of our smooth multipartite convex split
lemma, the quantum analogue of flattening plays
a crucial role in overcoming bottleneck due to non-commutativity of
certain operators involved in the proof.

Flattening ideas have been used in quantum information theory before.
Anshu and Jain
[
AJ22
]
take inspiration
from the classical
brothers construction and defines a unitary quantum flattening
operation. However in order to define their unitary, they need to
add a small ancilla in addition to the ‘brother’ Hilbert space. That
ancilla is initialised with a suitable
embezzling pure state
,
which is almost unchanged at the end of the unitary operation. Anshu
and Jain use this unitary in the context of one of their more
economical convex split lemmas.
The extra
ancilla containing the embezzling state does not hurt their application.
However, such an ancilla creates serious problems in our attempt to
flatten a quantumn state in order overcome the non-commutativity
bottleneck amongst certain operators in our smooth multipartite convex
split proof. Hence, we cannot use the unitary flattening operation of

[
AJ22
]
in this paper.

It turns out that our final solution for this problem is even simpler
than the unitary
flattening operation of
[
AJ22
]
. We take the vanilla brothers
construction outlined above and convert it in the most naive fashion into
a CPTP superoperator acting on quantum
states. Our flattening superoperator is not a unitary operation.
It does not use embezzling states.
As expected it is tailored to flatten a specific density matrix

\sigma^{A}
on the Hilbert space
A
. Serendipitously, it preserves the
fidelity between any two
quantum states
\rho_{1}^{A}
,
\rho_{2}^{A}
, though not the trace distance.
Nevertheless, this novel observation suffices to meet all our
requirements. We consider
this simple but powerful operation to be a major conceptual contribution
of this work.

We now describe our quantum flattening operation. Let
\sigma^{A}
be
a positive semidefinite operator on the Hilbert space
A
. Let
its eigenvalues and eigenvectors be

\{(\sigma(a,a),|a\rangle^{A})\}_{a}
. Let
0<\delta<1
. Let
F_{\sigma}

be the smallest positive integer such that there exist integers

\{f(a)\}_{a}
with the property that

\forall a:~~\sigma(a,a)\leq\frac{f(a)}{F_{\sigma}}\leq\sigma(a,a)(1+\delta).

Then the
flattening superoperator with respect to
\sigma^{A}
is
defined as

\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}(\rho^{A}):=\sum_{l=1}^{L}A_{l}^{A\rightarrow AL}(\rho^{A})(A_{l}^{\dagger})^{AL\rightarrow A},

where
\rho^{A}
is any Hermitian matrix on
A
,
L:=\max_{a}f(a)
and for
each
l\in[L]
,
A_{l}
is a linear map from the Hilbert space
A

to the Hilbert space
AL:=A\otimes\mathbb{C}^{L}
defined as:

A_{l}^{A\rightarrow AL}(|a\rangle^{A}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\frac{1}{\sqrt{f(a)}}|a\rangle^{A}|l\rangle^{L}&l\leq f(a),\\
&\\
0&l>f(a),\end{array}\right.

with the extension to the full vector space
A
by linearity.

To get a
better geometric picture of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}
, it helps
to order the
eigenvectors of
\sigma^{A}
as
\{|a_{1}\rangle,|a_{2}\rangle,\ldots,\}
in
non-decreasing order of
f(a)
. Introduce new notation
A(1)\equiv A
.
Consider fresh copies
A(l)
,
2\leq l\leq L
of the Hilbert space
A
.
We can then write

AL=A\otimes\mathbb{C}^{L}=\bigoplus_{l=1}^{L}A(l).

Define
f(a_{0})\equiv 0
.
For
i\in[|A|]
, define

A_{(i)}:=\spanning\{|a_{i}\rangle,|a_{i+1}\rangle,\ldots,|a_{|A|}\rangle\}.

For
i\in[A]
and
l\in[L]
, define
T_{i,l}
to be the identity
isometric embedding of
A_{(i)}
into
A(l)
. Depending on the
context,
T_{i,l}
can also be thought of as the identity superoperator
mapping matrices on
A_{(i)}
to matrices on
A(l)
.
Then,

\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{|A|}\bigoplus_{l=f(a_{i-1})+1}^{f(a_{i})}T_{i,l}^{A_{(i)}\rightarrow A(l)},

i.e.,
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}
is an orthogonal direct sum
of projection superoperators.

We now prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5

The superoperator
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}
is completely
positive and trace preserving.

Proof:

The Kraus representation of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}
given above
immediately implies it is completely positive. To check the trace
preserving property, we work in the basis of
A
given by the
eigenvectors of
\sigma^{A}
and obtain,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}A_{l}^{\dagger}A_{l}(a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle a|(\sum_{l=1}^{L}A_{l}^{\dagger}A_{l})|a^{\prime}\rangle\;=\;\sum_{l=1}^{\min\{f(a^{\prime}),f(a)\}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{f(a^{\prime})f(a)}}\langle a,l||a^{\prime},l\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\sum_{l=1}^{f(a)}\frac{1}{f(a)}\;=\;\delta_{aa^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{l=1}^{L}A_{l}^{\dagger}A_{l}(a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{A}.

This completes the proof of the lemma.

\square

Define the subspace
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
of
AL
by

F^{\prime}_{\sigma}:=\spanning\{|a\rangle^{A}|l\rangle^{L}:l\leq f(a)\}.

In other words, the image of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}
is contained
in the span of Hermitian operators with support in
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
.
The next lemma shows that the dimension of
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
is approximately

F_{\sigma}(\Tr\sigma)
.

Lemma 6

(\Tr\sigma)F_{\sigma}\leq|F^{\prime}_{\sigma}|\leq(1+\delta)(\Tr\sigma)F_{\sigma}.

Proof:

\displaystyle|F^{\prime}_{\sigma}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a}f(a)\;=\;F_{\sigma}\sum_{a}\frac{f(a)}{F_{\sigma}},

\displaystyle F_{\sigma}\sum_{a}\sigma(a,a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle F_{\sigma}\sum_{a}\frac{f(a)}{F_{\sigma}}\;\leq\;F_{\sigma}\sum_{a}\sigma(a,a)(1+\delta)

\displaystyle\implies F_{\sigma}(\Tr\sigma)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|F^{\prime}_{\sigma}|\;\leq\;(1+\delta)F_{\sigma}(\Tr\sigma).

This completes the proof of the lemma.

\square

We are now finally in a position to prove that

\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}
flattens
\sigma^{A}
.

Proposition 3

Let
\sigma^{A}
be a positive semidefinite operator on
A
. Then,

(1+\delta)^{-1}\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\sigma}}}{F_{\sigma}}\leq\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}(\sigma)\leq\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\sigma}}}{F_{\sigma}}.

Proof:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{A\rightarrow AL}_{\sigma}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\sigma(a,a)\mathcal{F}^{A\rightarrow AL}_{\sigma}(|a\rangle\langle a|)\;=\;\sum_{a}\sigma(a,a)\sum_{l=1}^{L}A_{l}|a\rangle\langle a|A_{l}^{\dagger}\;=\;\sum_{a}\frac{\sigma(a,a)}{f(a)}|a\rangle\langle a|^{A}\sum_{l=1}^{f(a)}|l\rangle\langle l|^{L}.

But,

\displaystyle\sigma(a,a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{f(a)}{F_{\sigma}}\;\leq\;(1+\delta)\sigma(a,a)

\displaystyle\implies\frac{(1+\delta)^{-1}}{F_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(a,a)}{f(a)}\;\leq\;\frac{1}{F_{\sigma}}.

This implies

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\delta)^{-1}}{F_{\sigma}}\sum_{a}|a\rangle\langle a|^{L}\sum_{l=1}^{f(a)}|l\rangle\langle l|^{L}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}(\sigma^{A})\;\leq\;\frac{1}{F_{\sigma}}\sum_{a}|a\rangle\langle a|^{L}\sum_{l=1}^{f(a)}|l\rangle\langle l|^{L}.

But,

\sum_{a}|a\rangle\langle a|^{L}\sum_{l=1}^{f(a)}|l\rangle\langle l|^{L}=\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\sigma}}.

This completes the proof of the proposition.

\square

We now show the important property that flattening does not change the
length preserving fidelity.

Proposition 4

Let
\sigma^{A}
,
\rho_{1}^{AB}
,
\rho_{2}^{AB}
be positive semidefinite
operators on their respective Hilbert spaces. Then,

F((\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow LA}\otimes\mathbb{I}^{B})(\rho_{1}^{AB}),(\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow LA}\otimes\mathbb{I}^{B})(\rho_{2}^{AB}))=F(\rho_{1}^{AB},\rho_{2}^{AB}).

Proof:

Consider the following unitary Stinespring dilation

U^{A\rightarrow ALL^{\prime}}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}
, where

ALL^{\prime}:=A\otimes\mathbb{C}^{L}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{L}
.

U:|a\rangle^{A}\mapsto|a\rangle^{A}\otimes\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{f(a)}}\sum_{l=1}^{f(a)}|l\rangle^{L}|l\rangle^{L^{\prime}}\right).

Define for
i=1,2
,

\sigma_{i}^{L^{\prime}LAB}:=(U^{A\rightarrow ALL^{\prime}}\otimes\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{B})\circ\rho_{i}^{AB}.

Since unitary superoperators preserve fidelity, we have

F(\rho_{1}^{AB},\rho_{2}^{AB})=F(\sigma_{1}^{L^{\prime}LAB},\sigma_{2}^{L^{\prime}LAB}).

Let
|\sigma_{1}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}
,
|\sigma_{2}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}
be
length preserving purifications of
\sigma_{1}^{L^{\prime}LAB}
,

\sigma_{2}^{L^{\prime}LAB}
achieving their fidelity i.e.

F(\sigma_{1}^{L^{\prime}LAB},\sigma_{2}^{L^{\prime}LAB})=F(\sigma_{1}^{L^{\prime}LABR},\sigma_{2}^{L^{\prime}LABR})=\langle{\sigma_{1}}|\sigma_{2}\rangle.

Then,

|\sigma_{1}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}|l\rangle^{L^{\prime}}|l\rangle^{L}|v_{1}(l)\rangle^{ABR},~~~|\sigma_{2}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}|l\rangle^{L^{\prime}}|l\rangle^{L}|v_{2}(l)\rangle^{ABR},

where
\{|v_{1}(l)\rangle^{ABR}\}_{l}
,
\{|v_{2}(l)\rangle^{ABR}\}_{l}
are in general
unnormalised, non-orthgonal pure states.

By the definition of the flattening operator
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
,

\sigma_{i}^{LAB}:=(\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{A\rightarrow AL}\otimes\mathbb{I}^{B})(\rho_{i}^{AB}),

i=1,2

have an orthogonal direct sum structure indexed by
l\in L
i.e.

\sigma_{i}^{LAB}=\bigoplus_{l=1}^{L}|l\rangle\langle l|^{L}\otimes\sigma_{i}(l)^{AB},

for some positive semidefinite operators
\sigma_{i}(l)^{AB}
. In other
words,
\sigma_{1}^{LAB}
,
\sigma_{2}^{LAB}
are classical on
L
.
As a result,

\||v_{i}(l)\rangle^{ABR}\|_{2}^{2}=\Tr[\sigma_{i}(l)^{AB}],

and
|v_{i}(l)\rangle^{ABR}
is a length preserving purification of

\sigma_{i}(l)^{AB}
. Moreover,

F(\sigma_{1}(l)^{AB},\sigma_{2}(l)^{AB})=\langle{v_{1}(l)}|v_{2}(l)\rangle.

Otherwise, the maximum overlap of length preserving purifications required
by the fidelity of positive semidefinite operators
\sigma_{1}^{LAB}
,

\sigma_{2}^{LAB}
will not be achieved.

We now investigate the fidelity of positive operators
\sigma_{1}^{LAB}
and

\sigma_{2}^{LAB}
.
Take
|\sigma_{1}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}
as the purification of

\sigma_{1}^{LAB}
.
Let
|\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}
be a fidelity achieving length preserving
purification of
\sigma_{2}^{LAB}
. Since the flattening operation is
trace preserving, we have

\Tr[\sigma_{i}^{LAB}]=\Tr[\rho_{i}^{AB}],

i=1,2
. Let

|\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}=\sum_{l^{\prime},l=1}^{L}|l^{\prime}\rangle^{L^{\prime}}|l\rangle^{L}|v_{2}(l^{\prime},l)\rangle^{ABR},

Then the unnormalised pure state

|\sigma^{\prime}_{2}(l)\rangle^{L^{\prime}ABR}:=\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{L}|l^{\prime}\rangle^{L^{\prime}}|v_{2}(l^{\prime},l)\rangle^{ABR}

is a length preserving purification of
\sigma_{2}(l)^{AB}
i.e.

\||\sigma_{2}^{\prime}(l)\rangle^{L^{\prime}ABR}\|_{2}^{2}=\Tr[\sigma_{2}(l)^{AB}].

One way to maximise the overlap between

|\sigma_{1}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}
and
|\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}
is to set

|v_{2}^{\prime}(l^{\prime},l)\rangle=0
if
l^{\prime}\neq l
and

|v_{2}^{\prime}(l,l)\rangle=|v_{2}(l)\rangle
.
In other words, we can take

|\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}=|\sigma_{2}\rangle^{L^{\prime}LABR}
.

Thus,

F(\sigma_{1}^{LAB},\sigma_{2}^{LAB})=\langle{\sigma_{1}}|\sigma_{2}\rangle=F(\sigma_{1}^{L^{\prime}LAB},\sigma_{2}^{L^{\prime}LAB})=F(\rho_{1}^{AB},\rho_{2}^{AB}),

completing the proof of the proposition.

\square

4
Fully smooth multipartite convex split via flattening

In this section we prove a smooth multipartite convex split lemma,
Lemma
7
,
using flattening, tilting and augmentation smoothing. At first glance,
Lemma
7
looks like
another version of the smooth multipartite convex split lemma proved
in
[
Sen24
]
, with the main difference being that
Lemma
7
states its
inner bounds stated in terms of
D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}
instead of

D^{\epsilon}_{2}
in
[
Sen24
]
. One may wonder about
the need of giving another proof of smooth multipartite convex split,
and that too a much longer and more complicated proof. There seems to be
no advantage in doing
so; the
D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}
formulation is not any stronger. In fact
it is likely
slightly worse than the
D^{\epsilon}_{2}
formulation. However, this
exercise
paves the way to prove a non pairwise independent smooth multipartite
soft covering
lemma for classical quantum states in the next section.
This is where our more sophisticated
machinery wins out as proving such a result is beyond the reach of
telescoping.
We will assume in this section, without loss of generality,
that the density matrix
\rho^{XYM}
in
the statement of convex split is normalised, by dividing by
\Tr\rho
.

We first state the version of the smooth multipartite convex split
lemma proved via flattening, tilting and augmentation smoothing.

Lemma 7

Let
\rho^{XYM}
be a subnormalised density matrix.
Let
\alpha^{X}
,
\beta^{Y}
be normalised
density matrices such that
\mathrm{supp}(\rho^{X})\leq\mathrm{supp}(\alpha^{X})
and

\mathrm{supp}(\rho^{Y})\leq\mathrm{supp}(\beta^{Y})
.
Define the
A
-fold tensor product states

\alpha^{X^{A}}:=(\alpha^{X})^{\otimes A},\beta^{Y^{B}}:=(\beta^{Y})^{\otimes B},

and the
(A-1)
-fold tensor product states
\alpha^{X^{-a}}
,

\beta^{Y^{-b}}
for any
a\in[A]
,
b\in[B]
.
Define the convex split state

\sigma^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}:=(AB)^{-1}\sum_{a=1}^{A}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\rho^{X_{a}Y_{b}M}\otimes\alpha^{X^{-a}}\otimes\beta^{Y^{-b}},

and the fully decoupled state

\tau^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}:=\alpha^{X^{A}}\otimes\beta^{Y^{B}}\otimes\rho^{M}.

Suppose

\displaystyle\log A

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\rho^{M})+\log\epsilon^{-1/20},

\displaystyle\log B

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{YM}\|\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})+\log\epsilon^{-1/20},

\displaystyle\log A+\log B

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XYM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})+\log\epsilon^{-1/20}.

Then,

\|\sigma^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}-\tau^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}\|_{1}<10\epsilon^{1/64}(\Tr\rho).

We now proceed with the proof of Lemma
7
.
From the definitions of smooth
D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\cdot\|\cdot)
,
we have subnormalised density matrices
\rho^{XYM}(3)
,
\rho^{XM}(1)

and
\rho^{YM}(2)
satisfying

\|\rho^{XYM}-\rho^{XYM}(3)\|_{1}\leq\epsilon,~~\|\rho^{XM}-\rho^{XM}(1)\|_{1}\leq\epsilon,~~\|\rho^{YM}-\rho^{YM}(2)\|_{1}\leq\epsilon,

(10)

such that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rho^{XYM}(3)&\leq&2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XYM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}(\alpha^{X}\otimes\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M}),\\
\rho^{XM}(1)&\leq&2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\rho^{M})}(\alpha^{X}\otimes\rho^{M}),\\
\rho^{YM}(2)&\leq&2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{YM}\|\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}(\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M}).\end{array}

(11)

Apply the flattening CPTP superoperator to get the normalised
density matrix

\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}:=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{X\rightarrow L_{X}X}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\beta}^{Y\rightarrow L_{Y}Y}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\rho}^{M\rightarrow L_{M}M})(\rho^{XYM}).

Observe that

\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{X\rightarrow L_{X}X}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\rho}^{M\rightarrow L_{M}M})(\rho^{XM}),~~\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}=(\mathcal{F}_{\beta}^{Y\rightarrow L_{Y}Y}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\rho}^{M\rightarrow L_{M}M})(\rho^{YM}).

Define
\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)
,
\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)
,

\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)
via flattening in the natural fashion. Then
we use Equation
10
and conclude,

\begin{array}[]{c}\|\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}-\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)\|_{1}<\epsilon,\\
\|\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}-\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)\|_{1}<\epsilon,~~\|\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}-\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)\|_{1}<\epsilon,\end{array}

(12)

since a CPTP superoperator cannot increase the trace distance.

By Lemma
6
and Proposition
3
,
we have

\begin{array}[]{c}(1+\delta)^{-2}\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\alpha}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}|}\leq\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{X\rightarrow L_{X}X}(\alpha^{X})\leq(1+\delta)\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\alpha}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}|},\\
(1+\delta)^{-2}\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\beta}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\beta}|}\leq\mathcal{F}_{\beta}^{Y\rightarrow L_{Y}Y}(\beta^{Y})\leq(1+\delta)\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\beta}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\beta}|},\\
(1+\delta)^{-2}\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\rho}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\rho}|}.\mathcal{F}_{\rho}^{M\rightarrow L_{M}M}(\rho^{M})\leq(1+\delta)\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\rho}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\rho}|}.\end{array}

(13)

Because a CPTP superoperator respects the Löwner partial order on
Hermitian matrices, combining Equations
11
and

13
gives us

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)&\leq&(1+\delta)^{3}2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XYM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\alpha}}\otimes\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\beta}}\otimes\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\rho}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}||F^{\prime}_{\beta}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|},\\
\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)&\leq&(1+\delta)^{2}2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\rho^{M})}\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\alpha}}\otimes\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\rho}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|},\\
\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)&\leq&(1+\delta)^{2}2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{YM}\|\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\beta}}\otimes\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\rho}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\beta}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|}.\end{array}

(14)

In particular,

\mathrm{supp}(\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)\leq F^{\prime}_{\alpha}\otimes F^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes F^{\prime}_{\rho},~\mathrm{supp}(\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)\leq F^{\prime}_{\alpha}\otimes F^{\prime}_{\rho},~\mathrm{supp}(\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)\leq F^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes F^{\prime}_{\rho}.

Applying Proposition
2
to
Equations
12
,
14
tells us that
there exists orthogonal
projectors
\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)
,
\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)
,

\Pi^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)
such that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Tr[\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}]&\geq&1-\sqrt{\epsilon},\\
\Tr[\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}]&\geq&1-\sqrt{\epsilon},\\
\Tr[\Pi^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}]&\geq&1-\sqrt{\epsilon},\\
\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}&=&\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3),\\
\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}&=&\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1),\\
\Pi^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}&=&\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}\Pi^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2),\\
\|\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}(3)\circ\rho^{L_{X}XL_{Y}YL_{M}M}\|_{\infty}&\leq&\frac{(1+\delta)^{3}(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XYM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}||F^{\prime}_{\beta}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|},\\
\|\Pi^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}(1)\circ\rho^{L_{X}XL_{M}M}\|_{\infty}&\leq&\frac{(1+\delta)^{2}(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\rho^{M})}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|},\\
\|\Pi^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}(2)\circ\rho^{L_{Y}YL_{M}M}\|_{\infty}&\leq&\frac{(1+\delta)^{2}(1+2\sqrt{\epsilon})2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{YM}\|\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}}{|F^{\prime}_{\beta}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|}.\end{array}

(15)

Propositions
3
and

4
, and Lemma
6

taken together allow us to prove Lemma
8

below. Lemma
8

will be used as the first step in the proof of
Lemma
7
.
We note that
\delta
can be taken as small as we please
at the expense of increasing
|F^{\prime}_{\sigma}|
. Essentially,
\delta
can be
treated as a free parameter.

Lemma 8

Let
A
,
B
be positive integers.
Let
\alpha^{X}
,
\beta^{Y}
,
\rho^{M}
,
\sigma^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}

be normalised density matrices.
Define the normalised density matrix

\tau^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}:=(\alpha^{X})^{\otimes A}\otimes(\beta^{Y})^{\otimes B}\otimes\rho^{M}.

Then,

\displaystyle F(((\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{X\rightarrow L_{X}X})^{\otimes A}\otimes(\mathcal{F}_{\beta}^{Y\rightarrow L_{Y}Y})^{\otimes B}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\rho}^{M\rightarrow L_{M}M})(\sigma^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}),

\displaystyle~~~~~~~((\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{X\rightarrow L_{X}X})^{\otimes A}\otimes(\mathcal{F}_{\beta}^{Y\rightarrow L_{Y}Y})^{\otimes B}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\rho}^{M\rightarrow L_{M}M})(\tau^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(\sigma^{X^{A}Y^{B}M},\tau^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}),

\displaystyle(1+\delta)^{-(A+B+1)}\left(\left(\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\alpha}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}|}\right)^{\otimes A}\otimes\left(\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\beta}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\beta}|}\right)^{\otimes B}\otimes\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\rho}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\rho}|}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle((\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}^{X\rightarrow L_{X}X})^{\otimes A}\otimes(\mathcal{F}_{\beta}^{Y\rightarrow L_{Y}Y})^{\otimes B}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\rho}^{M\rightarrow L_{M}M})(\tau^{X^{A}Y^{B}M})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+\delta)^{A+B+1}\left(\left(\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\alpha}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}|}\right)^{\otimes A}\otimes\left(\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\beta}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\beta}|}\right)^{\otimes B}\otimes\frac{\hbox{\small 1\kern-3.8pt\normalsize 1}^{F^{\prime}_{\rho}}}{|F^{\prime}_{\rho}|}\right),

where
L_{X}
,
L_{Y}
,
L_{M}
are the additional Hilbert spaces required for
the respective flattening superoperators.

In order to prepare for the proof of
Lemma
7
,
we will assume that the states
\sigma^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}
and
\tau^{X^{A}Y^{B}M}

have been flattened as in Lemma
8
, where
the positive integers
A
,
B
come from the statement of
Lemma
7
.
We will call the flattened states

\sigma^{(L_{X}X)^{A}(L_{Y}Y)^{B}L_{M}M}
and

\tau^{(L_{X}X)^{A}(L_{Y}Y)^{B}L_{M}M}
. We will choose the
positive integers
F_{\alpha}
,
F_{\beta}
,
F_{\rho}
large enough, and
correspondingly the dimensions of
L_{X}
,
L_{Y}
,
L_{M}
large enough so
that
\delta\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2(A+B+1)}
. Also to make the
notation lighter, henceforth we will always work with the flattened
states and the Hilbert spaces
L_{X}X
,
L_{Y}Y
and
L_{M}M
. Hence from
now on we redefine
X\equiv L_{X}X
,
Y\equiv L_{Y}Y
and

M\equiv L_{M}M
. Under this redefinition, we restate
Equation
15
as
Equation
16
below. That is, we can assume that there
are projectors
\Pi^{XYM}(3)
,
\Pi^{XM}(1)
and
\Pi^{YM}(2)
such
that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Tr[\Pi^{XYM}(3)\rho^{XYM}]&\geq&1-\sqrt{\epsilon},\\
\Tr[\Pi^{XM}(1)\rho^{XM}]&\geq&1-\sqrt{\epsilon},\\
\Tr[\Pi^{YM}(2)\rho^{YM}]&\geq&1-\sqrt{\epsilon},\\
\Pi^{XYM}(3)\rho^{XYM}&=&\rho^{XYM}\Pi^{XYM}(3),\\
\Pi^{XM}(1)\rho^{XM}&=&\rho^{XM}\Pi^{XM}(1),\\
\Pi^{YM}(2)\rho^{YM}&=&\rho^{YM}\Pi^{YM}(2),\\
\|\Pi^{XYM}(3)\rho^{XYM}\|_{\infty}&\leq&\frac{(1+3\sqrt{\epsilon})2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XYM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}||F^{\prime}_{\beta}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|},\\
\|\Pi^{XM}(1)\rho^{XM}\|_{\infty}&\leq&\frac{(1+3\sqrt{\epsilon})2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{XM}\|\alpha^{X}\otimes\rho^{M})}}{|F^{\prime}_{\alpha}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|},\\
\|\Pi^{YM}(2)\rho^{YM}\|_{\infty}&\leq&\frac{(1+3\sqrt{\epsilon})2^{D^{\epsilon}_{\infty}(\rho^{YM}\|\beta^{Y}\otimes\rho^{M})}}{|F^{\prime}_{\beta}||F^{\prime}_{\rho}|}\\
F^{\prime}_{\alpha}&\leq&X,~~F^{\prime}_{\beta}\;\leq\;Y,~~F^{\prime}_{\rho}\;\leq\;M,\\
